<p><b>Millard Fillmore</b> (January 7, 1800 – March 8, 1874) was the 13th <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a>, serving from 1850 to 1853, the last to be a member of the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a> while in the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>.  A former member of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._House_of_Representatives" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a> from <a href="/wiki/Upstate_New_York" title="Upstate New York">upstate New York</a>, Fillmore was elected as the 12th <a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">vice president</a> in <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848</a>, and succeeded to the presidency in July 1850 upon the death of <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a>. Fillmore was instrumental in passing the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a>, a bargain that led to a brief truce in the battle over the expansion of <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slavery</a>. He failed to win the <a href="/wiki/1852_Whig_National_Convention" title="1852 Whig National Convention">Whig nomination for president in 1852</a> but gained the endorsement of the nativist <a href="/wiki/Know_Nothing_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Know Nothing Party">Know Nothing Party</a> four years later and finished third in the <a href="/wiki/1856_United_States_presidential_election" title="1856 United States presidential election">1856 presidential election</a>.
</p><p>Fillmore was born into poverty in the <a href="/wiki/Finger_Lakes" title="Finger Lakes">Finger Lakes</a> area of upstate New York. His parents were tenant farmers during his formative years. Though he had little formal schooling, he studied diligently to become a lawyer. He became prominent in the <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo</a> area as an attorney and politician, and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Assembly" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Assembly">New York Assembly</a> in 1828 and to the House of Representatives in 1832. Fillmore initially belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a>, but became a member of the Whig Party as formed in the mid-1830s. He was a rival for the state party leadership with the editor <a href="/wiki/Thurlow_Weed" title="Thurlow Weed">Thurlow Weed</a> and his protégé <a href="/wiki/William_H._Seward" title="William H. Seward">William H. Seward</a>. Throughout his career, Fillmore declared slavery an evil but said it was beyond the federal government's power to end it. Seward was openly hostile to slavery and argued that the federal government had a role to play in ending it. Fillmore was an unsuccessful candidate for <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the United States House of Representatives">Speaker of the U.S. House of Representatives</a> when the Whigs took control of the chamber in 1841, but was made chairman of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_Ways_and_Means" title="United States House Committee on Ways and Means">Ways and Means Committee</a>. Defeated in bids for the Whig nomination for vice president in 1844 and for <a href="/wiki/1844_New_York_state_election" title="1844 New York state election">New York governor</a> the same year, Fillmore was elected <a href="/wiki/Comptroller_of_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Comptroller of New York">Comptroller of New York</a> in 1847, the first to hold that post by direct election.
</p><p>As vice president, Fillmore was largely ignored by Taylor; even in dispensing patronage in New York, Taylor consulted Weed and Seward. But in his capacity as president of the Senate, Fillmore presided over its angry debates, as the <a href="/wiki/31st_United_States_Congress" title="31st United States Congress">31st Congress</a> decided whether to allow slavery in the <a href="/wiki/Mexican_Cession" title="Mexican Cession">Mexican Cession</a>. Unlike Taylor, Fillmore supported <a href="/wiki/Omnibus_bill#Historical_examples" title="Omnibus bill">Henry Clay's omnibus bill</a>, the basis of the 1850 Compromise. Upon becoming president in July 1850, he dismissed <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor#Presidency_(1849–1850)" title="Zachary Taylor">Taylor's cabinet</a> and pushed Congress to pass the compromise. The <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1850" title="Fugitive Slave Act of 1850">Fugitive Slave Act</a>, expediting the return of escaped slaves to those who claimed ownership, was a controversial part of the compromise. Fillmore felt duty-bound to enforce it, though it damaged his popularity and also the Whig Party, which was torn between its Northern and Southern factions. In foreign policy, he supported <a href="/wiki/Perry_Expedition" title="Perry Expedition">U.S. Navy expeditions to open trade in Japan</a>, opposed <a href="/wiki/Second_French_Empire" title="Second French Empire">French</a> designs on <a href="/wiki/Hawaiian_Kingdom" title="Hawaiian Kingdom">Hawaii</a>, and was embarrassed by <a href="/wiki/Narciso_L%C3%B3pez" title="Narciso López">Narciso López</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Filibuster_(military)" title="Filibuster (military)">filibuster</a> expeditions to <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba#The_19th_century" title="Captaincy General of Cuba">Cuba</a>. Fillmore sought the Whig nomination for a full term in 1852 but was passed over in favor of <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a>.
</p><p>As the Whig Party broke up after Fillmore's presidency, he and many in its conservative wing joined the Know Nothings and formed the American Party. Despite his party's emphasis on anti-immigration and anti-Catholic policies, during his 1856 candidacy, he said little about immigration, focusing on the preservation of the Union, and won only <a href="/wiki/Maryland" title="Maryland">Maryland</a>. During the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>, Fillmore denounced secession and agreed that the Union must be maintained by force if necessary, but was critical of <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>'s war policies. After peace was restored, he supported President <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Reconstruction_era" title="Reconstruction era">Reconstruction</a> policies. Fillmore remained involved in civic interests in retirement, including as chancellor of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Buffalo" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Buffalo">University of Buffalo</a>, which he had helped found in 1846. Historians consistently <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">rank</a> Fillmore among the worst presidents in American history, largely for his policies regarding slavery, as well as among the <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#Memorability_of_the_presidents" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">least memorable</a>. His association with the Know Nothings and support of Johnson's reconstruction policies further tarnished his reputation and legacy.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_career">Early life and career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Millard Fillmore was born on January 7, 1800, in a <a href="/wiki/Log_cabin" title="Log cabin">log cabin</a>, on a farm in what is now <a href="/wiki/Moravia,_New_York" title="Moravia, New York">Moravia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cayuga_County,_New_York" title="Cayuga County, New York">Cayuga County</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Finger_Lakes" title="Finger Lakes">Finger Lakes</a> region of <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a>. His parents were Phoebe Millard and <a href="/wiki/Nathaniel_Fillmore" title="Nathaniel Fillmore">Nathaniel Fillmore</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and he was the second of eight children and the oldest son.<sup id="cite_ref-pre_president_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pre_president-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nathaniel Fillmore was the son of Nathaniel Fillmore Sr. (1739–1814), a native of <a href="/wiki/Franklin,_Connecticut" title="Franklin, Connecticut">Franklin, Connecticut</a>, who became one of the earliest settlers of <a href="/wiki/Bennington,_Vermont" title="Bennington, Vermont">Bennington, Vermont</a>, when it was founded in the territory that was then called the <a href="/wiki/New_Hampshire_Grants" title="New Hampshire Grants">New Hampshire Grants</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nathaniel Fillmore and Phoebe Millard moved from Vermont in 1799 and sought better opportunities than were available on Nathaniel's stony farm, but the title to their Cayuga County land proved defective, and the Fillmore family moved to nearby <a href="/wiki/Sempronius,_New_York" title="Sempronius, New York">Sempronius</a>, where they leased land as <a href="/wiki/Tenant_farmer" title="Tenant farmer">tenant farmers</a>, and Nathaniel occasionally taught school.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback191–197_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback191–197-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The historian <a href="/wiki/Tyler_Anbinder" title="Tyler Anbinder">Tyler Anbinder</a> described Fillmore's childhood as "one of hard work, frequent privation, and virtually no formal schooling."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmorebirth.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Historical marker at the site of Fillmore&#39;s birth" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Fillmorebirth.jpg/220px-Fillmorebirth.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Fillmorebirth.jpg/330px-Fillmorebirth.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Fillmorebirth.jpg/440px-Fillmorebirth.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Historical marker at the site of Fillmore's birth in Cayuga County, New York</figcaption></figure>
<p>Over time Nathaniel became more successful in Sempronius, but during Millard's formative years, the family endured severe poverty.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Nathaniel became sufficiently regarded that he was chosen to serve in local offices, including justice of the peace.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Hoping that his oldest son would learn a trade, he convinced Millard, who was 14, not to enlist for the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry18_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry18-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Apprentice" class="mw-redirect" title="Apprentice">apprenticed</a> him to clothmaker Benjamin Hungerford in <a href="/wiki/Sparta,_New_York" title="Sparta, New York">Sparta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore was relegated to menial labor, and unhappy at not learning any skills, he left Hungerford's employ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry19_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry19-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His father then placed him in the same trade at a mill in <a href="/wiki/Niles,_New_York" title="Niles, New York">New Hope</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry20_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry20-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Seeking to better himself, Millard bought a share in a <a href="/wiki/Circulating_library" title="Circulating library">circulating library</a> and read all the books that he could.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry20_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry20-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> In 1819 he took advantage of idle time at the mill to enroll at a new academy in the town, where he met a classmate, <a href="/wiki/Abigail_Fillmore" title="Abigail Fillmore">Abigail Powers</a>, and fell in love with her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback224–258_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback224–258-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later in 1819 Nathaniel moved the family to Montville, a hamlet of Moravia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry22_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry22-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Appreciating his son's talents, Nathaniel followed his wife's advice and persuaded Judge Walter Wood, the Fillmores' landlord and the wealthiest person in the area, to allow Millard to be his law clerk for a trial period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry23_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry23-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Wood agreed to employ young Fillmore and to supervise him as he <a href="/wiki/Read_law" class="mw-redirect" title="Read law">read law</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry23_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry23-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore earned money teaching school for three months and bought out his mill apprenticeship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry24_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry24-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He left Wood after eighteen months; the judge had paid him almost nothing, and both quarreled after Fillmore had, unaided, earned a small sum by advising a farmer in a minor lawsuit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry25_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry25-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Refusing to pledge not to do so again, Fillmore gave up his clerkship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback258–308_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback258–308-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Nathaniel again moved the family, and Millard accompanied it west to <a href="/wiki/East_Aurora,_New_York" title="East Aurora, New York">East Aurora</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Erie_County,_New_York" title="Erie County, New York">Erie County</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> where Nathaniel purchased a farm that became prosperous.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1821 Fillmore turned 21, reaching <a href="/wiki/Age_of_majority" title="Age of majority">adulthood</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry26_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry26-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He taught school in East Aurora and accepted a few cases in justice of the peace courts, which did not require the practitioner to be a licensed attorney.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry26_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry26-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He moved to Buffalo the following year and continued his study of law, first while he taught school and then in the law office of Asa Rice and Joseph Clary. Meanwhile, he also became engaged to Abigail Powers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry26_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry26-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In 1823 he was <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_bar_in_the_United_States" title="Admission to the bar in the United States">admitted to the bar</a>, declined offers from Buffalo law firms, and returned to East Aurora to establish a practice as the town's only resident lawyer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry528–34_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry528–34-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Later in life, Fillmore said he had initially lacked the self-confidence to practice in the larger city of Buffalo. His biographer, <a href="/wiki/Paul_Finkelman" title="Paul Finkelman">Paul Finkelman</a>, suggested that after being under others' thumbs all his life, Fillmore enjoyed the independence of his East Aurora practice.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5–6_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman5–6-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Millard and Abigail wed on February 5, 1826. They had two children, <a href="/wiki/Millard_Powers_Fillmore" title="Millard Powers Fillmore">Millard Powers Fillmore</a> (1828–1889) and <a href="/wiki/Mary_Abigail_Fillmore" title="Mary Abigail Fillmore">Mary Abigail Fillmore</a> (1832–1854).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry128–134_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry128–134-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Buffalo_politician">Buffalo politician</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Buffalo politician">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Other members of the Fillmore family were active in politics and government in addition to Nathaniel's service as a justice of the peace.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> Millard also became interested in politics, and the rise of the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a> in the late 1820s provided his entry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_House.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of a yellow house with white porch pillars and a center door with a window on either side" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Fillmore_House.jpg/220px-Fillmore_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Fillmore_House.jpg/330px-Fillmore_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Fillmore_House.jpg/440px-Fillmore_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2077" data-file-height="1731" /></a><figcaption>Millard Fillmore helped build <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore_House" class="mw-redirect" title="Millard Fillmore House">this house</a> in <a href="/wiki/East_Aurora,_New_York" title="East Aurora, New York">East Aurora, New York</a>, and lived there from 1826 to 1830.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="width: 230px; float: right; clear: right; margin:0 0 1.5em 1.5em"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:115%">External video</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="text-align: left"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="video icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/16px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/24px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/32px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="128" data-file-height="128" /></span></span> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.c-span.org/video/?66854-1/president-fillmore-home">Tour of Millard Fillmore House Museum, East Aurora, New York, August 19, 1995</a>, <a href="/wiki/C-SPAN" title="C-SPAN">C-SPAN</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Many Anti-Masons were opposed to the presidential candidacy of General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>, who was a <a href="/wiki/Mason_(Freemasonry)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mason (Freemasonry)">Mason</a>. Fillmore was a delegate to the New York convention that endorsed President <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> for re-election and also served at two Anti-Masonic conventions in the summer of 1828.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> At the conventions, Fillmore and one of the early political bosses, the newspaper editor <a href="/wiki/Thurlow_Weed" title="Thurlow Weed">Thurlow Weed</a>, met and impressed each other.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore was the leading citizen in East Aurora, having successfully sought election to the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Assembly" title="New York State Assembly">New York State Assembly</a>, and served in <a href="/wiki/Albany,_New_York" title="Albany, New York">Albany</a> for three one-year terms (1829 to 1831).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore's 1828 election contrasted the victories of the <a href="/wiki/Jacksonian_democracy" title="Jacksonian democracy">Jacksonian Democrats</a> (soon <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">the Democrats</a>), who swept the general into the White House and their party to a majority in Albany and so Fillmore was in the minority in the Assembly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry42_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry42-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He proved effective anyway by promoting legislation to provide court witnesses the option of taking a non-religious oath and, in 1830, abolishing <a href="/wiki/Imprisonment_for_debt" class="mw-redirect" title="Imprisonment for debt">imprisonment for debt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry18_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry18-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> By then much of Fillmore's legal practice was in Buffalo, and later that year he moved there with his family. He did not seek re-election in 1831.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman12–13-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith45_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith45-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore was also successful as a lawyer. Buffalo was then rapidly expanding, recovering from British conflagration during the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>, and becoming the western terminus of the <a href="/wiki/Erie_Canal" title="Erie Canal">Erie Canal</a>. Court cases from outside Erie County began falling to Fillmore's lot, and he reached prominence as a lawyer in Buffalo before he moved there. He took his lifelong friend <a href="/wiki/Nathan_K._Hall" title="Nathan K. Hall">Nathan K. Hall</a> as a law clerk in East Aurora. Hall later became Fillmore's partner in Buffalo and his <a href="/wiki/Postmaster_General_of_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Postmaster General of the United States">postmaster general</a> during Fillmore's presidency. Buffalo was legally a village when Fillmore arrived, and although the bill to incorporate it as a city passed the legislature after he had left the Assembly, Fillmore helped draft the city charter.
</p><p>In addition to his legal practice Fillmore helped found the Buffalo High School Association, joined the <a href="/wiki/Lyceum_movement" title="Lyceum movement">lyceum</a>, attended the local <a href="/wiki/Unitarianism" title="Unitarianism">Unitarian</a> church, and became a leading citizen of Buffalo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback314,_750–810_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback314,_750–810-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> He was also active in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Army_National_Guard" title="New York Army National Guard">New York Militia</a> and attained the rank of <a href="/wiki/Major_(United_States)" title="Major (United States)">major</a> as inspector of the 47th Brigade.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Millard_Fillmore_Papers&#39;&#39;_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Millard_Fillmore_Papers&#39;&#39;-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Representative">Representative</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Representative">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term_and_return_to_Buffalo">First term and return to Buffalo</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: First term and return to Buffalo">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1832 Fillmore ran successfully for the <a href="/wiki/U.S._House_of_Representatives" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a>. The Anti-Masonic presidential candidate, <a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a>, a former attorney general, won only Vermont, and President Jackson easily gained re-election. At the time, Congress convened its annual session in December and so Fillmore had to wait more than a year after his election to take his seat. Fillmore, Weed, and others realized that opposition to Masonry was too narrow a foundation to build a national party. They formed the broad-based <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a> from <a href="/wiki/National_Republican_Party" title="National Republican Party">National Republicans</a>, Anti-Masons, and disaffected Democrats. The Whigs were initially united by their opposition to Jackson but became a major party by expanding their platform to include support for economic growth through rechartering the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> and federally-funded <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a>, including roads, bridges, and canals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry936–940,_993–999_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry936–940,_993–999-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Weed had joined the Whigs before Fillmore and became a power within the party, and Weed's anti-slavery views were stronger than those of Fillmore, who disliked slavery but considered the federal government powerless over it. They were closer to those of another prominent New York Whig, <a href="/wiki/William_H._Seward" title="William H. Seward">William H. Seward</a> of <a href="/wiki/Auburn,_New_York" title="Auburn, New York">Auburn</a>, who was also seen as a Weed protégé.<sup id="cite_ref-pre_president_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pre_president-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Washington Fillmore urged the expansion of Buffalo harbor, a decision under federal jurisdiction, and he privately lobbied Albany for the expansion of the state-owned Erie Canal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback878–905_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback878–905-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Even during the 1832 campaign, Fillmore's affiliation as an Anti-Mason had been uncertain, and he rapidly shed the label once sworn in. Fillmore came to the notice of the influential Massachusetts Senator <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a>, who took the new representative under his wing. Fillmore became a firm supporter, and they continued their close relationship until Webster's death late in Fillmore's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman13_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman13-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Despite Fillmore's support of the Second Bank as a means for national development, he did not speak in the congressional debates in which some advocated renewing its charter although Jackson had vetoed legislation for a charter renewal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1261_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1261-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore supported building infrastructure by voting in favor of navigation improvements on the <a href="/wiki/Hudson_River" title="Hudson River">Hudson River</a> and constructing a bridge across the <a href="/wiki/Potomac_River" title="Potomac River">Potomac River</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry999_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry999-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Anti-Masonry was still strong in Western New York though it was petering out nationally. When the Anti-Masons did not nominate him for a second term in 1834, Fillmore declined the Whig nomination, seeing that the two parties would split the anti-Jackson vote and elect the Democrat. Despite Fillmore's departure from office, he was a rival for the state party leadership with Seward, the unsuccessful 1834 Whig <a href="/wiki/Gubernatorial" class="mw-redirect" title="Gubernatorial">gubernatorial</a> candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman14_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman14-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore spent his time out of office building his law practice and boosting the Whig Party, which gradually absorbed most of the Anti-Masons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1079_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1079-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> By 1836 Fillmore was confident enough of anti-Jackson unity that he accepted the Whig nomination for Congress. Democrats, led by their presidential candidate, Vice President <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a>, were victorious nationwide and in Van Buren's home state of New York, but Western New York voted Whig and sent Fillmore back to Washington.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1495–1508_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1495–1508-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_to_fourth_terms">Second to fourth terms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Second to fourth terms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Van Buren, faced with the economic <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a>, which was caused partly by the lack of confidence in private banknote issues after Jackson had instructed the government <a href="/wiki/Specie_Circular" title="Specie Circular">to accept only gold or silver</a>, called a special session of Congress. Government money had been held in so-called "<a href="/wiki/Pet_banks" title="Pet banks">pet banks</a>" since Jackson had withdrawn it from the Second Bank. Van Buren proposed to place funds in sub-treasuries, government depositories that would not lend money. Believing that government funds should be lent to develop the country, Fillmore felt it would lock the nation's limited supply of gold money away from commerce. Van Buren's sub-treasury and other economic proposals passed, but as hard times continued, the Whigs saw an increased vote in the 1837 elections and captured the New York Assembly, which set up a fight for the 1838 gubernatorial nomination. Fillmore supported the leading Whig vice-presidential candidate from 1836, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Granger" title="Francis Granger">Francis Granger</a>, but Weed preferred Seward.
</p><p>Fillmore was embittered when Weed got the nomination for Seward but campaigned loyally, Seward was elected, and Fillmore won another term in the House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1556–1679_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1556–1679-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The rivalry between Fillmore and Seward was affected by the growing anti-slavery movement. Although Fillmore disliked slavery, he saw no reason for it to be a political issue. Seward, however, was hostile to slavery and made it clear in his actions as governor by refusing to return slaves claimed by Southerners.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1556–1679_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1556–1679-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> When the Buffalo bar proposed Fillmore for the position of vice-chancellor of the eighth judicial district in 1839, Seward refused, nominated <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Whittlesey" title="Frederick Whittlesey">Frederick Whittlesey</a>, and indicated that if the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Senate" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Senate">New York Senate</a> rejected Whittlesey he still would not appoint Fillmore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1326–1331_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1326–1331-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore was active in the discussions of presidential candidates which preceded the <a href="/wiki/1839_Whig_National_Convention" title="1839 Whig National Convention">Whig National Convention</a> for the 1840 race. He initially supported General <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a> but really wanted to defeat Kentucky Senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>, a slaveholder who he felt could not carry New York State. Fillmore did not attend the convention but was gratified when it nominated General <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> for president, with former Virginia Senator <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a> his running mate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1356–1361_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1356–1361-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore organized Western New York for <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison_presidential_campaign,_1840" class="mw-redirect" title="William Henry Harrison presidential campaign, 1840">the Harrison campaign</a>, and the national ticket was elected, and Fillmore easily gained a fourth term in the House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1891_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1891-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the urging of Clay, Harrison quickly called a special session of Congress. With the Whigs able to organize the House for the first time, Fillmore sought the Speakership, but it went to a Clay acolyte, <a href="/wiki/John_White_(Kentucky_politician)" title="John White (Kentucky politician)">John White</a> of Kentucky.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1950–1957_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1950–1957-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Fillmore was made chairman of the <a href="/wiki/House_Ways_and_Means_Committee" class="mw-redirect" title="House Ways and Means Committee">House Ways and Means Committee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Harrison was expected to go along with anything Clay and other congressional Whig leaders proposed, but Harrison died on April 4, 1841. Vice President Tyler was elevated to the presidency; the onetime maverick Democrat soon broke with Clay over congressional proposals for a national bank to stabilize the currency, which he vetoed twice and so was expelled from the Whig Party. Fillmore remained on the fringes of that conflict by generally supporting the congressional Whig position, but his chief achievement as Ways and Means chairman was the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1842" title="Tariff of 1842">Tariff of 1842</a>. The existing tariff did not protect manufacturing, and part of the revenue was distributed to the states, a decision made in better times that was now depleting the Treasury. Fillmore prepared a bill raising tariff rates that was popular in the country, but the continuation of distribution assured Tyler's veto and much political advantage for the Whigs. A House committee, headed by Massachusetts's <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a>, condemned Tyler's actions. Fillmore prepared a second bill, now omitting distribution. When it reached Tyler's desk, he signed it but, in the process, offended his erstwhile Democratic allies. Thus Fillmore not only achieved his legislative goal but also managed to isolate Tyler politically.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback1957–2186_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback1957–2186-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore received praise for the tariff, but in July 1842 he announced he would not seek re-election. The Whigs nominated him anyway, but he refused the nomination. Tired of Washington life and the conflict that had revolved around Tyler, Fillmore sought to return to his life and law practice in Buffalo. He continued to be active in the <a href="/wiki/Lame_duck_session" class="mw-redirect" title="Lame duck session">lame duck session</a> of Congress that followed the 1842 elections and returned to Buffalo in April 1843. According to his biographer, Scarry, "Fillmore concluded his Congressional career at a point when he had become a powerful figure, an able statesman at the height of his popularity."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1729–1776_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1729–1776-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Weed deemed Fillmore "able in debate, wise in council, and inflexible in his political sentiments".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1766_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1766-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="National_figure">National figure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: National figure">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Out of office, Fillmore continued his law practice and made long-neglected repairs to his Buffalo home. He remained a major political figure and led the committee that welcomed John Quincy Adams to Buffalo. The former president expressed his regret at Fillmore's absence from the halls of Congress. Some urged Fillmore to run for vice president with Clay, the consensus Whig choice for president in 1844. <a href="/wiki/Horace_Greeley" title="Horace Greeley">Horace Greeley</a> wrote privately that "my own first choice has long been Millard Fillmore," and others thought Fillmore should try to win back the governor's mansion for the Whigs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry1776–1820_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry1776–1820-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Seeking to return to Washington, Fillmore wanted the vice presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2417_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2417-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Portrait of Millard Fillmore dressed largely in black facing the artist" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="265" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-Unidentified_Artist_-_Millard_Fillmore_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4774" data-file-height="5745" /></a><figcaption>Fillmore <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1843</span>, artist unknown</figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore hoped to gain the endorsement of the New York delegation to the national convention, but Weed wanted the vice presidency for Seward, with Fillmore as governor. Seward, however, withdrew before the <a href="/wiki/1844_Whig_National_Convention" title="1844 Whig National Convention">1844 Whig National Convention</a>. When Weed's replacement vice presidential hopeful, <a href="/wiki/Willis_Hall_(New_York_politician)" title="Willis Hall (New York politician)">Willis Hall</a>, fell ill, Weed sought to defeat Fillmore's candidacy to force him to run for governor. Weed's attempts to boost Fillmore as a gubernatorial candidate caused the latter to write, "I am not willing to be treacherously killed by this pretended kindness<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... do not suppose for a minute that I think they desire my nomination for governor."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2425–2471_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2425–2471-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> New York sent a delegation to the convention in Baltimore pledged to support Clay but with no instructions as to how to vote for vice president. Weed told out-of-state delegates that the New York party preferred to have Fillmore as its gubernatorial candidate, and after Clay was nominated for president, the second place on the ticket fell to former New Jersey senator <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Frelinghuysen" title="Theodore Frelinghuysen">Theodore Frelinghuysen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2471–2486_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2471–2486-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Putting a good face on his defeat, Fillmore met and publicly appeared with Frelinghuysen and quietly spurned Weed's offer to get him nominated as governor at the state convention. Fillmore's position in opposing slavery only at the state level made him acceptable as a statewide Whig candidate, and Weed saw to it the pressure on Fillmore increased. Fillmore had stated that a convention had the right to draft anyone for political service, and Weed got the convention to choose Fillmore, who had broad support, despite his reluctance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2486–2536_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2486–2536-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Democrats nominated Senator <a href="/wiki/Silas_Wright" title="Silas Wright">Silas Wright</a> as their gubernatorial candidate and former Tennessee Governor <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> for president. Although Fillmore worked to gain support among <a href="/wiki/German-Americans" class="mw-redirect" title="German-Americans">German-Americans</a>, a major constituency, he was hurt among immigrants by the fact that in New York City, Whigs had supported a <a href="/wiki/Nativism_(politics)" title="Nativism (politics)">nativist</a> candidate in the mayoral election earlier in 1844, and Fillmore and his party were tarred with that brush.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2536–2562_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2536–2562-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He was not friendly to immigrants and blamed his defeat on "foreign Catholics".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman24_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman24-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Clay was beaten as well.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2486–2536_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2486–2536-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore's biographer Paul Finkelman suggested that Fillmore's hostility to immigrants and his weak position on slavery had defeated him for governor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman23–24_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman23–24-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1846 Fillmore was involved in the founding of what is now the <a href="/wiki/University_at_Buffalo" title="University at Buffalo">University at Buffalo</a> (earlier the University of Buffalo), became its first <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_(education)" title="Chancellor (education)">chancellor</a>, and served until his death in 1874. He had opposed the <a href="/wiki/Annexation_of_Texas" class="mw-redirect" title="Annexation of Texas">annexation of Texas</a>, spoke against the subsequent <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a>, and saw the war as a contrivance to extend slavery's realm. Fillmore was angered when President Polk vetoed a river and harbors bill that would have benefited Buffalo,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman35,_152_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman35,_152-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> and he wrote, "May God save the country for it is evident the people will not."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2620_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2620-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> At the time, New York governors served a two-year term, and Fillmore could have had the Whig nomination in 1846 had he wanted it. He actually came within one vote of it while he maneuvered to get the nomination for his supporter, <a href="/wiki/John_Young_(governor)" title="John Young (governor)">John Young</a>, who was elected. A new constitution for New York State provided the office of <a href="/wiki/Comptroller_of_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Comptroller of New York">comptroller</a> to be made elective, as were the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Attorney_General" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Attorney General">attorney general</a> and some other positions that were formerly chosen by the state legislature. Fillmore's work in finance as the Ways and Means chairman made him an obvious candidate for comptroller, and he was successful in getting the Whig nomination for the 1847 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2735–2763_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2735–2763-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> With a united party at his back, Fillmore won by 38,000 votes, the largest margin that a Whig candidate for statewide office would ever achieve in New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman25_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman25-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before moving to Albany to take office on January 1, 1848, he had left his law firm and rented out his house. Fillmore received positive reviews for his service as comptroller. In that office he was a member of the state canal board, supported its expansion, and saw that it was managed competently. He secured an enlargement of Buffalo's canal facilities. The comptroller regulated the banks, and Fillmore stabilized the currency by requiring that state-chartered banks keep New York and federal bonds to the value of the banknotes they issued. <a href="/wiki/National_Bank_Act" title="National Bank Act">A similar plan</a> was adopted by Congress in 1864.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2769–2799_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2769–2799-64">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1848">Election of 1848</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Election of 1848">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 United States presidential election</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nomination">Nomination</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Nomination">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For further information on the procedures of American political conventions, see <a href="/wiki/United_States_presidential_nominating_convention" title="United States presidential nominating convention">United States presidential nominating convention</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MFillmore-congressman.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Black and white engraved portrait of Fillmore" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/MFillmore-congressman.jpg/220px-MFillmore-congressman.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="252" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/MFillmore-congressman.jpg/330px-MFillmore-congressman.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/MFillmore-congressman.jpg/440px-MFillmore-congressman.jpg 2x" data-file-width="692" data-file-height="793" /></a><figcaption>Engraving of Fillmore</figcaption></figure>
<p>President Polk had pledged not to seek a second term, and with gains in Congress during the 1846 election cycle, the Whigs were hopeful of taking the White House in 1848. The party's perennial candidates, Henry Clay and Daniel Webster, both wanted the nomination and amassed support from congressional colleagues. Many rank-and-file Whigs backed the Mexican War hero, General <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a>, for president. Although Taylor was extremely popular, many Northerners had qualms about electing a Louisiana slaveholder at a time of sectional tension over whether slavery should be allowed in the territories that had been ceded by Mexico. Taylor's uncertain political views gave others pause: his career in the Army had prevented him from ever casting a ballot for president though he stated that he was a Whig supporter. Some feared that they might elect another Tyler, or another Harrison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman43–45_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman43–45-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TaylorFillmore.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A color campaign banner with portraits of Zachary Taylor on the left and Fillmore on the right" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/220px-TaylorFillmore.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/330px-TaylorFillmore.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/440px-TaylorFillmore.jpg 2x" data-file-width="617" data-file-height="439" /></a><figcaption>Taylor (left) – Fillmore campaign banner by <a href="/wiki/Nathaniel_Currier" title="Nathaniel Currier">Nathaniel Currier</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>With the nomination undecided, Weed maneuvered for New York to send an uncommitted delegation to the <a href="/wiki/1848_Whig_National_Convention" title="1848 Whig National Convention">1848 Whig National Convention</a> in Philadelphia in the hope of being a <a href="/wiki/Kingmaker" title="Kingmaker">kingmaker</a> in a position to place ex-Governor Seward on the ticket or to get him a high federal office. He persuaded Fillmore to support an uncommitted ticket but did not tell the Buffalonian of his hopes for Seward. Weed was an influential editor with whom Fillmore tended to co-operate for the greater good of the Whig Party. However, Weed had sterner opponents, including Governor Young, who disliked Seward and did not want to see him gain high office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2902–2955_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2902–2955-66">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite Weed's efforts, Taylor was nominated on the fourth ballot, to the anger of Clay's supporters and of <a href="/wiki/Conscience_Whig" class="mw-redirect" title="Conscience Whig">Conscience Whigs</a> from the Northeast. When order had been restored, <a href="/wiki/John_A._Collier" title="John A. Collier">John A. Collier</a>, a New Yorker who opposed Weed, addressed the convention. Delegates hung on his every word as he described himself as a Clay partisan; he had voted for Clay on each ballot. He eloquently described the grief of the Clay supporters, frustrated again in their battle to make Clay president. Collier warned of a fatal breach in the party and said that only one thing could prevent it: the nomination of Fillmore for vice president, whom he depicted incorrectly as a strong Clay supporter. Fillmore actually agreed with many of Clay's positions but did not back him for president and was not in Philadelphia. Delegates did not know what Collier had said was false or at least greatly exaggerated and there was a large reaction in Fillmore's favor. At the time, the presidential candidate did not automatically pick his running mate, and despite the efforts of Taylor's managers to get the nomination for their choice, <a href="/wiki/Abbott_Lawrence" title="Abbott Lawrence">Abbott Lawrence</a> of Massachusetts, Fillmore became the Whig nominee for vice president on the second ballot.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback2981–2994_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback2981–2994-67">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Weed had wanted the vice-presidential nomination for Seward, who attracted few delegate votes, and Collier had acted to frustrate them in more ways than one, since with the New Yorker Fillmore as vice president, under the political customs of the time, no one from that state could be named to the Cabinet. Fillmore was accused of complicity in Collier's actions, but that was never substantiated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3001–3008_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3001–3008-68">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, there were sound reasons for Fillmore's selection, as he was a proven vote-getter from electorally-crucial New York, and his track record in Congress and as a candidate showed his devotion to Whig doctrine, allaying fears he might be another Tyler were something to happen to General Taylor. Delegates remembered him for his role in the Tariff of 1842, and he had been mentioned as a vice-presidential possibility, along with Lawrence and Ohio's <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Ewing" title="Thomas Ewing">Thomas Ewing</a>. His rivalry with Seward, who was already known for anti-slavery views and statements, made Fillmore more acceptable in the South.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman47–49_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman47–49-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnyder37_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder37-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1848.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Color map showing the election results by state" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="General_election">General election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: General election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It was customary in the mid-19th century for a candidate for high office not to appear to seek it. Thus, Fillmore remained at the comptroller's office in Albany and made no speeches. The 1848 campaign was conducted in the newspapers and with addresses made by surrogates at rallies. The Democrats nominated Senator <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> of Michigan for president, with General <a href="/wiki/William_O._Butler" class="mw-redirect" title="William O. Butler">William O. Butler</a> as his running mate, but it became a three-way fight since the <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a>, which opposed the spread of slavery, chose ex-President Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry3138–3150_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry3138–3150-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> There was a crisis among the Whigs when Taylor also accepted the presidential nomination of a group of dissident South Carolina Democrats. Fearing that Taylor would be a party <a href="/wiki/Apostasy" title="Apostasy">apostate</a> like Tyler, Weed in late August scheduled a rally in Albany aimed at electing an uncommitted slate of presidential electors. Fillmore interceded with the editor and assured him that Taylor was loyal to the party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman53_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman53-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry3188–3245_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry3188–3245-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Northerners assumed that Fillmore, hailing from a free state, was an opponent of the spread of slavery. Southerners accused him of being an abolitionist, which he hotly denied.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman51_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman51-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore responded to one Alabamian in a widely published letter that slavery was an evil, but the federal government had no authority over it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman53_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman53-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Taylor and Fillmore corresponded twice in September, with Taylor happy that the crisis over the South Carolinians was resolved. Fillmore assured his running mate that the electoral prospects for the ticket looked good, especially in the Northeast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry3245–3258_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry3245–3258-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the end the Taylor-Fillmore ticket won narrowly, with New York's electoral votes again key to the election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3090_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3090-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The Whig ticket won the popular vote by 1,361,393 (47.3%) to 1,223,460 (42.5%) and triumphed 163 to 127 in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College" title="United States Electoral College">Electoral College</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> Minor party candidates took no electoral votes,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry3283_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry3283-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> but the strength of the burgeoning anti-slavery movement was shown by the vote for Van Buren, who won no states but earned 291,501 votes (10.1%) and finished second in New York, Vermont, and Massachusetts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman51–52_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman51–52-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
 <div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span id="Vice_presidency_.281849.E2.80.931850.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Vice_presidency_(1849–1850)">Vice presidency (1849–1850)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Vice presidency (1849–1850)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Daguerreotype portrait of Fillmore in 1849" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg/220px-Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg/330px-Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg/440px-Millard_Fillmore_daguerreotype_by_Mathew_Brady_1849.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1052" data-file-height="1254" /></a><figcaption>Fillmore photographed in 1849 by <a href="/wiki/Mathew_Brady" title="Mathew Brady">Mathew Brady</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore was sworn in as vice president on March 5, 1849, in the <a href="/wiki/Old_Senate_Chamber" title="Old Senate Chamber">Senate Chamber</a>. Since March 4 (which was then <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Inauguration Day">Inauguration Day</a>) fell on a Sunday, the swearing-in was postponed to the following day. Fillmore took the oath from <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States" title="Chief Justice of the United States">Chief Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Roger_B._Taney" title="Roger B. Taney">Roger B. Taney</a> and, in turn, swore in the senators beginning their terms, including Seward, who had <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate_election_in_New_York,_1849" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Senate election in New York, 1849">been elected by the New York legislature</a> in February.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3101–3307_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3101–3307-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore had spent the four months between the election and the swearing-in being feted by the New York Whigs and winding up affairs in the comptroller's office. Taylor had written to him and promised influence in the new administration. The <a href="/wiki/President-elect_of_the_United_States" title="President-elect of the United States">president-elect</a> mistakenly thought that the vice president was a cabinet member, which was not true in the 19th century. Fillmore, Seward and Weed had met and come to a general agreement on how to divide federal jobs in New York. Once he went to Washington, Seward made friendly contact with Taylor's cabinet nominees, advisers, and the general's brother. An alliance between the incoming administration and the Weed machine was soon under way behind Fillmore's back. In exchange for support, Seward and Weed were allowed to designate who was to fill federal jobs in New York, and Fillmore was given far less influence than had been agreed. When Fillmore discovered that after the election, he went to Taylor, which only made the warfare against Fillmore's influence more open. Fillmore's supporters such as Collier, who had nominated him at the convention, were passed over for candidates backed by Weed, who was triumphant even in Buffalo. That greatly increased Weed's influence in New York politics and diminished Fillmore's. According to Rayback, "by mid-1849, Fillmore's situation had become desperate."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3101–3307_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3101–3307-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Despite his lack of influence, office-seekers pestered him, as did those with a house to lease or sell since there was no official vice-presidential residence at the time. He enjoyed one aspect of his office because of his lifelong love of learning: he became deeply involved in the administration of the <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a> as a member <i><a href="/wiki/Ex_officio" class="mw-redirect" title="Ex officio">ex officio</a></i> of its Board of Regents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith160–162_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith160–162-82">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Through 1849, slavery was an unresolved issue in the territories. Taylor advocated the admission of California and New Mexico,<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> which were both likely to outlaw slavery. Southerners were surprised to learn the president, despite being a Southern slaveholder, did not support the introduction of slavery into the new territories, as he believed the institution could not flourish in the arid Southwest. There was anger across party lines in the South, where making the territories free of slavery was considered to be the exclusion of Southerners from part of the national heritage. When Congress met in December 1849, the discord was manifested in the election for Speaker, which took weeks and dozens of ballots to resolve, as the House divided along sectional lines.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3307–3367_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3307–3367-84">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith93–94_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith93–94-85">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore countered the Weed machine by building a network of like-minded Whigs in New York State. With backing from wealthy New Yorkers, their positions were publicized by the establishment of a rival newspaper to Weed's <i>Albany Evening Journal</i>. All pretense at friendship between Fillmore and Weed vanished in November 1849 when they happened to meet in New York City and exchanged accusations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback3367–3399_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback3367–3399-86">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="An engraving of Henry Clay speaking in the Old Senate Chamber with Fillmore seated in the upper right" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg/300px-Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg/450px-Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg/600px-Henry_Clay_Senate3_crop.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2096" data-file-height="1868" /></a><figcaption>From a <a href="/wiki/Peter_F._Rothermel" title="Peter F. Rothermel">Peter F. Rothermel</a> engraving: Vice President Fillmore (upper right) presides over the Compromise debates as <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> takes the floor of the <a href="/wiki/Old_Senate_Chamber" title="Old Senate Chamber">Old Senate Chamber</a>. <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a> (seen in part standing just to Fillmore's right) and <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a> (seated to the left of Clay) look on.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore presided<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> over some of the most momentous and passionate debates in American history as the Senate debated whether to allow slavery in the territories. The ongoing sectional conflict had already excited much discussion when on January 21, 1850, President Taylor sent a special message to Congress that urged the admission of California immediately and New Mexico later and for the Supreme Court to settle the boundary dispute whereby the state of Texas claimed much of what is now the state of New Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry3445–3467_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry3445–3467-88">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> On January 29, Clay introduced his "<a href="/wiki/Omnibus_bill#Historical_examples" title="Omnibus bill">Omnibus Bill</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup> which would give victories to both North and South by admitting California as a free state, organizing territorial governments in New Mexico and <a href="/wiki/Utah_Territory" title="Utah Territory">Utah</a>, and banning the slave trade in the <a href="/wiki/District_of_Columbia" class="mw-redirect" title="District of Columbia">District of Columbia</a>. The bill would also toughen the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1793" title="Fugitive Slave Act of 1793">Fugitive Slave Act</a>, as resistance to enforcement in parts of the North had been a longtime Southern grievance. Clay's bill provided for the settlement of the Texas-New Mexico boundary dispute, and the status of slavery in the territories would be decided by those living there, the concept being known as <a href="/wiki/Popular_sovereignty" title="Popular sovereignty">popular sovereignty</a>. Taylor was unenthusiastic about the bill, which languished in Congress. After hearing weeks of debate, however, Fillmore informed him in May 1850 that if senators divided equally on the bill, he would cast his tie-breaking vote in favor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore did his best to keep the peace among the senators and reminded them of the vice president's power to rule them out of order, but he was blamed for failing to maintain the peace when a physical confrontation between Mississippi's <a href="/wiki/Henry_S._Foote" title="Henry S. Foote">Henry S. Foote</a> and Missouri's <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hart_Benton_(politician)" title="Thomas Hart Benton (politician)">Thomas Hart Benton</a> broke out on April 17. Before other senators intervened to separate them, Foote pointed a gun at his colleague as Benton advanced on him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith138–139,_163–165_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith138–139,_163–165-90">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281850.E2.80.931853.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1850–1853)">Presidency (1850–1853)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Presidency (1850–1853)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Millard_Fillmore" title="Presidency of Millard Fillmore">Presidency of Millard Fillmore</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Succession_amid_crisis">Succession amid crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Succession amid crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright">
<div class="thumbinner" style="width: 232px;">
<div class="thumbimage" style="width: 230px; height: 295px; overflow: hidden;">
<div style="position: relative; top: -50px; left: -43px; width: 325px"><div class="noresize"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:FILLMORE,_Millard-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="BEP-engraved portrait of Fillmore as president" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/325px-FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="325" height="427" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/488px-FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/650px-FILLMORE%2C_Millard-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="8050" data-file-height="10568" /></a></span></div></div>
</div>
<div class="thumbcaption">
<div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:FILLMORE,_Millard-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" title="File:FILLMORE, Millard-President (BEP engraved portrait).jpg"> </a></div><a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing" title="Bureau of Engraving and Printing">BEP</a>-engraved portrait of Fillmore as president
</div>
</div>
</div>
<p>July 4, 1850 was a very hot day in Washington, and President Taylor, who attended the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_of_July" class="mw-redirect" title="Fourth of July">Fourth of July</a> ceremonies to lay the cornerstone of the <a href="/wiki/Washington_Monument" title="Washington Monument">Washington Monument</a>, refreshed himself, likely with cold milk and cherries. What he consumed likely gave him <a href="/wiki/Gastroenteritis" title="Gastroenteritis">gastroenteritis</a>, and he died on July 9. Taylor, nicknamed "Old Rough and Ready", had gained a reputation for toughness through his military campaigning in the heat, and his sudden death came as a shock to the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman1_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman1-91">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore had been called from his chair presiding over the Senate on July 8 and had sat with members of the cabinet in a vigil outside Taylor's bedroom at the White House. He received the formal notification of the president's death, signed by the cabinet, on the evening of July 9 in his residence at the <a href="/wiki/Willard_Hotel" class="mw-redirect" title="Willard Hotel">Willard Hotel</a>. After acknowledging the letter and spending a sleepless night,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnyder43_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder43-92">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore went to the House of Representatives, where, at a <a href="/wiki/Joint_session_of_the_United_States_Congress" title="Joint session of the United States Congress">joint session of Congress</a>, he took the oath as president from <a href="/wiki/William_Cranch" title="William Cranch">William Cranch</a>, the chief judge of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Circuit_Court_of_the_District_of_Columbia" title="United States Circuit Court of the District of Columbia">federal court for the District of Columbia</a>, who had also sworn in President Tyler. The cabinet officers, as was customary when a new president took over, submitted their resignations but expected Fillmore to refuse and to allow them to continue in office. Fillmore had been marginalized by the cabinet members, and he accepted the resignations though he asked them to stay on for a month, which most refused to do. Fillmore is the only president who succeeded by death or resignation not to retain, at least initially, his predecessor's cabinet. He was already in discussions with Whig leaders and, on July 20, began to send new nominations to the Senate, with the Fillmore Cabinet to be led by Webster as Secretary of State. Webster had outraged his Massachusetts constituents by supporting Clay's bill and, with his Senate term to expire in 1851, had no political future in his home state. Fillmore appointed his old law partner, Nathan Hall, as Postmaster General, a cabinet position that controlled many patronage appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman72–77_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman72–77-93">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The new department heads were mostly supporters of the Compromise, like Fillmore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson48_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson48-94">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The brief pause from politics out of national grief at Taylor's death did not abate the crisis. Texas had attempted to assert its authority in New Mexico, and the state's governor, <a href="/wiki/Peter_H._Bell" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter H. Bell">Peter H. Bell</a>, had sent belligerent letters to President Taylor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith152–157_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith152–157-95">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore received another letter after he had become president. He reinforced federal troops in the area and warned Bell to keep the peace.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson48_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson48-94">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By July 31 Clay's bill was effectively dead, as all significant provisions other than the organization of Utah Territory had been removed by amendment. As one wag put it, the "Mormons" were the only remaining passengers on the omnibus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith158–160_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith158–160-96">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Illinois Senator <a href="/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas" title="Stephen A. Douglas">Stephen A.
