Douglas</a> then stepped to the fore, with Clay's agreement, proposing to break the omnibus bill into individual bills that could be passed piecemeal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith158–160_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith158–160-96">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore endorsed that strategy, which eventually divided the compromise into five bills.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore sent a special message to Congress on August 6, 1850; disclosed the letter from Governor Bell and his reply; warned that armed Texans would be viewed as intruders; and urged Congress to defuse sectional tensions by passing the Compromise. Without the presence of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Triumvirate" title="Great Triumvirate">Great Triumvirate</a> of <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a>, Webster, and Clay, who had long dominated the Senate,<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;i&#93;</a></sup> Douglas and others were able to lead the Senate towards the administration-backed package of bills. Each bill passed the Senate with the support of the section that wanted it, with a few members who were determined to see all the bills passed. The battle then moved to the House, which had a Northern majority because of the population. Most contentious was the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1850" title="Fugitive Slave Act of 1850">Fugitive Slave Bill</a>, whose provisions were anathema to abolitionists. Fillmore applied pressure to get Northern Whigs, including New Yorkers, to abstain, rather than to oppose the bill. Through the legislative process, various changes were made, including the setting of a boundary between <a href="/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory" title="New Mexico Territory">New Mexico Territory</a> and Texas, the state being given a payment to settle any claims. <a href="/wiki/An_Act_for_the_Admission_of_the_State_of_California" title="An Act for the Admission of the State of California">California was admitted</a> as a free state, the District of Columbia's slave trade was ended, and the final status of slavery in New Mexico and Utah would be settled later. Fillmore signed the bills as they reached his desk and held the Fugitive Slave Bill for two days until he received a favorable opinion as to its constitutionality from the new Attorney General, <a href="/wiki/John_J._Crittenden" title="John J. Crittenden">John J. Crittenden</a>. Although some Northerners were unhappy at the Fugitive Slave Act, relief was widespread in the hope of settling the slavery question.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry4025–4102_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry4025–4102-98">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman82–85_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman82–85-99">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Poster warning that the Boston police enforce the Fugitive Slave Act" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg/150px-Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="222" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg/225px-Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg/300px-Slave_kidnap_post_1851_boston.jpg 2x" data-file-width="346" data-file-height="512" /></a><figcaption></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_affairs">Domestic affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Domestic affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Fugitive Slave Act remained contentious after its enactment. Southerners complained bitterly about any leniency in its application, but its enforcement was highly offensive to many Northerners. Abolitionists recited the inequities of the law since anyone aiding an escaped slave was punished severely, and it granted no due process to the escapee, who could not testify before a magistrate. The law also permitted a higher payment to the hearing magistrate for deciding the escapee was a slave, rather than a free man. Nevertheless, Fillmore believed himself bound by his oath as president and by the bargain that had been made in the Compromise to enforce the Fugitive Slave Act. He did so even though some prosecutions or attempts to return slaves ended badly for the government, with acquittals and the slave <a href="/wiki/Shadrach_Minkins" title="Shadrach Minkins">taken from federal custody</a> and freed by a Boston mob. Such cases were widely publicized North and South, inflamed passions in both places, and undermined the good feeling that had followed the Compromise.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith208–13_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith208–13-100">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1850 the social reformer <a href="/wiki/Dorothea_Dix" title="Dorothea Dix">Dorothea Dix</a> wrote to Fillmore to urge support of her proposal in Congress for land grants to finance asylums for the impoverished mentally ill. Though her proposal did not pass, they became friends, met in person, and continued to correspond well after Fillmore's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnyder80–82_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder80–82-101">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1850 Fillmore appointed <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> leader <a href="/wiki/Brigham_Young" title="Brigham Young">Brigham Young</a> as the first governor of <a href="/wiki/Utah_Territory" title="Utah Territory">Utah Territory</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In gratitude, Young named the first territorial capital "<a href="/wiki/Fillmore,_Utah" title="Fillmore, Utah">Fillmore</a>" and the surrounding county "<a href="/wiki/Millard_County,_Utah" title="Millard County, Utah">Millard</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A longtime supporter of national infrastructure development, Fillmore signed bills to subsidize the <a href="/wiki/Illinois_Central" class="mw-redirect" title="Illinois Central">Illinois Central</a> railroad from Chicago to <a href="/wiki/Mobile,_Alabama" title="Mobile, Alabama">Mobile</a>, and for <a href="/wiki/Soo_Locks" title="Soo Locks">a canal</a> at <a href="/wiki/Sault_Ste._Marie,_Michigan" title="Sault Ste. Marie, Michigan">Sault Ste. Marie</a>. The 1851 completion of the <a href="/wiki/Erie_Railroad" title="Erie Railroad">Erie Railroad</a> in New York prompted Fillmore and his cabinet to ride the first train from New York City to the shores of Lake Erie, in the company with many other politicians and dignitaries. Fillmore made many speeches along the way from the train's rear platform, urged acceptance of the Compromise, and later went on a tour of New England with his Southern cabinet members. Although Fillmore urged Congress to authorize a <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_railroad" title="Transcontinental railroad">transcontinental railroad</a>, it did not do so until a decade later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199–200_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199–200-104">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore appointed one justice to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court of the United States</a> and made four appointments to <a href="/wiki/United_States_district_court" title="United States district court">United States district courts</a>, including that of his law partner and cabinet officer, Nathan Hall, to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Northern_District_of_New_York" title="United States District Court for the Northern District of New York">federal district court in Buffalo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> When Supreme Court Justice <a href="/wiki/Levi_Woodbury" title="Levi Woodbury">Levi Woodbury</a> died in September 1851 with the Senate not in session, Fillmore made a <a href="/wiki/Recess_appointment" title="Recess appointment">recess appointment</a> of <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Robbins_Curtis" title="Benjamin Robbins Curtis">Benjamin Robbins Curtis</a> to the Court. In December, with Congress convened, Fillmore formally nominated Curtis, who was confirmed. In 1857 Justice Curtis dissented from the Court's decision in the slavery case of <i><a href="/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford" title="Dred Scott v. Sandford">Dred Scott v. Sandford</a></i> and resigned as a matter of principle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith218,_247_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith218,_247-106">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Justice <a href="/wiki/John_McKinley" title="John McKinley">John McKinley</a>'s death in 1852 led to repeated fruitless attempts by the president to fill the vacancy. The Senate took no action on the nomination of the <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a> attorney <a href="/wiki/Edward_A._Bradford" title="Edward A. Bradford">Edward A. Bradford</a>. Fillmore's second choice, <a href="/wiki/George_Edmund_Badger" title="George Edmund Badger">George Edmund Badger</a>, asked for his name to be withdrawn. Senator-elect <a href="/wiki/Judah_P._Benjamin" title="Judah P. Benjamin">Judah P. Benjamin</a> declined to serve. The nomination of <a href="/wiki/William_C._Micou" title="William C. Micou">William C. Micou</a>, a New Orleans lawyer recommended by Benjamin, was not acted on by the Senate. The vacancy was finally filled after Fillmore's term, when President <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Pierce" title="Franklin Pierce">Franklin Pierce</a> nominated <a href="/wiki/John_Archibald_Campbell" title="John Archibald Campbell">John Archibald Campbell</a>, who was confirmed by the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-judicial_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-judicial-107">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Foreign relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mfillmore.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Official White House Portrait of Fillmore, standing and looking to his right" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Mfillmore.jpeg/220px-Mfillmore.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="355" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Mfillmore.jpeg/330px-Mfillmore.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Mfillmore.jpeg/440px-Mfillmore.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1861" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Official portrait of Fillmore by <a href="/wiki/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy" title="George Peter Alexander Healy">George Peter Alexander Healy</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1857</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore oversaw two highly-competent Secretaries of State, Daniel Webster, and after the New Englander's 1852 death, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Everett" title="Edward Everett">Edward Everett</a>. Fillmore looked over their shoulders and made all major decisions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith233_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith233-108">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> He was particularly active in Asia and the Pacific, especially with regard to Japan, which then still <a href="/wiki/Sakoku" title="Sakoku">prohibited nearly all foreign contact</a>. American merchants and shipowners wanted Japan "opened up" for trade, which would allow commerce and permit American ships to call there for food and water and in emergencies without them being punished. They were concerned that American sailors cast away on the Japanese coast were imprisoned as criminals.<sup id="cite_ref-Foreign_Affairs_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foreign_Affairs-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore and Webster dispatched <a href="/wiki/Commodore_(USN)" class="mw-redirect" title="Commodore (USN)">Commodore</a> <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Perry_(naval_officer)" class="mw-redirect" title="Matthew Perry (naval officer)">Matthew C. Perry</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Perry_Expedition" title="Perry Expedition">Perry Expedition</a> to open Japan to relations with the outside world. Perry and his ships reached Japan in July 1853, four months after the end of Fillmore's term.<sup id="cite_ref-Foreign_Affairs_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foreign_Affairs-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore was a staunch opponent of European influence in Hawaii. France, under Emperor <a href="/wiki/Napoleon_III" title="Napoleon III">Napoleon III</a>, sought to annex Hawaii but backed down after Fillmore issued a strongly-worded message warning that "the United States would not stand for any such action."<sup id="cite_ref-Foreign_Affairs_109-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foreign_Affairs-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor had pressed Portugal for payment of American claims dating as far back as the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a> and had refused offers of arbitration, but Fillmore gained a favorable settlement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith72–73_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith72–73-110">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore had difficulties regarding <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba" title="Captaincy General of Cuba">Cuba</a> since many Southerners hoped to see the island as an American slave territory. Cuba was a Spanish slave colony.<sup id="cite_ref-Foreign_Affairs_109-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foreign_Affairs-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Venezuelan adventurer <a href="/wiki/Narciso_L%C3%B3pez" title="Narciso López">Narciso López</a> recruited Americans for three <a href="/wiki/Filibuster_(military)" title="Filibuster (military)">filibustering</a> expeditions to Cuba in the hope of overthrowing Spanish rule. After the second attempt in 1850, López and some of his followers were indicted for breach of the <a href="/wiki/Neutrality_Act_of_1794" title="Neutrality Act of 1794">Neutrality Act</a> but were quickly acquitted by friendly Southern juries.<sup id="cite_ref-Foreign_Affairs_109-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foreign_Affairs-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> The final López expedition ended with his execution by the Spanish, who put several Americans before the firing squad, including the nephew of Attorney General Crittenden. That resulted in riots against the Spanish in New Orleans, which caused their consul to flee. The historian <a href="/wiki/Elbert_B._Smith" title="Elbert B. Smith">Elbert B. Smith</a>, who wrote of the Taylor and the Fillmore presidencies, suggested that Fillmore could have had war against Spain had he wanted. Instead, Fillmore, Webster, and the Spanish worked out a series of face-saving measures that settled the crisis without armed conflict. Many Southerners, including Whigs, supported the filibusters, and Fillmore's response helped to divide his party as the 1852 election approached.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith228_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith228-111">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A much-publicized event of the Fillmore presidency was the late 1851 arrival of <a href="/wiki/Lajos_Kossuth" title="Lajos Kossuth">Lajos Kossuth</a>, the exiled leader of a failed <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1848" title="Hungarian Revolution of 1848">Hungarian revolution</a> against Austria. Kossuth wanted the United States to recognize Hungary's independence. Many Americans were sympathetic to the Hungarian rebels, especially recent German immigrants, who were now coming in large numbers and had become a major political force. Kossuth was feted by Congress, and Fillmore allowed a White House meeting after he had received word that Kossuth would not try to politicize it. Despite his promise, Kossuth made a speech promoting his cause. The American enthusiasm for Kossuth petered out, and he departed for Europe. Fillmore refused to change the American policy of remaining neutral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith230–232_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith230–232-112">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1852_and_completion_of_term">Election of 1852 and completion of term</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Election of 1852 and completion of term">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1852_Whig_National_Convention" title="1852 Whig National Convention">1852 Whig National Convention</a></div>
<p>As the <a href="/wiki/1852_United_States_presidential_election" title="1852 United States presidential election">election of 1852</a> approached, Fillmore remained undecided on whether to run for a full term as president. Secretary Webster had long coveted the presidency and was past 70 but planned a final attempt to gain the White House. Fillmore, sympathetic to the ambitions of his longtime friend, issued a letter in late 1851 stating that he did not seek a full term, but Fillmore was reluctant to rule it out for fear the party would be captured by the Sewardites. Thus, approaching the <a href="/wiki/1852_Whig_National_Convention" title="1852 Whig National Convention">national convention</a> in Baltimore, to be held in June 1852, the major candidates were Fillmore, Webster, and General Scott. Weed and Seward backed Scott. In late May, the Democrats nominated former New Hampshire senator Franklin Pierce, who had been out of federal politics for nearly a decade before 1852 but had a profile that had risen by his military service during the Mexican War. His nomination as a Northerner sympathetic to the southern view on slavery united the Democrats and meant that the Whig candidate would face an uphill battle to gain the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith238–244_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith238–244-113">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore had become unpopular with northern Whigs for signing and enforcing the Fugitive Slave Act but still had considerable support from the South, where he was seen as the only candidate capable of uniting the party. Once the convention passed a <a href="/wiki/Party_platform" title="Party platform">party platform</a> endorsing the Compromise as a final settlement of the slavery question, Fillmore was willing to withdraw. He found that many of his supporters could not accept Webster and that his action would nominate Scott. The convention was deadlocked until Saturday, June 19, when a total of 46 ballots had been taken, and the delegates adjourned until Monday. Party leaders proposed a deal to Fillmore and Webster: if the latter could increase his vote total over the next several ballots, enough Fillmore supporters would go along to put him over the top. Otherwise, Webster would withdraw in favor of Fillmore. The President quickly agreed, but Webster did not do so until Monday morning. On the 48th ballot, Webster delegates began to defect to Scott, and the general gained the nomination on the 53rd ballot. Webster was far more unhappy at the outcome than was Fillmore, who refused the secretary's resignation. Without the votes of much of the South and also of Northerners who depended on peaceful intersectional trade, Scott was easily beaten by Pierce in November. Smith suggested that the Whigs might have done much better with Fillmore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith244–247_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith244–247-114">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The final months of Fillmore's term were uneventful. Webster died in October 1852, but during his final illness, Fillmore effectively acted as his own Secretary of State without incident, and Everett stepped competently into Webster's shoes. Fillmore intended to lecture Congress on the slavery question in his final <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_Union" title="State of the Union">annual message</a> in December but was talked out of it by his cabinet, and he contented himself with pointing out the prosperity of the nation and expressing gratitude for the opportunity to serve it. There was little discussion of slavery during the lame-duck session of Congress, and Fillmore left office on March 4, 1853, to be succeeded by Pierce.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith247–249_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith247–249-115">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281853.E2.80.931874.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1853–1874)">Post-presidency (1853–1874)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Post-presidency (1853–1874)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_tragedies">Personal tragedies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Personal tragedies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Fillmore was the first president to return to private life without independent wealth or the possession of a landed estate. With no pension to anticipate, he needed to earn a living and felt that it should be in a way that would uphold the dignity of his former office. His friend Judge Hall assured him it would be proper for him to practice law in the higher courts of New York, and Fillmore so intended.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback5726–5745_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback5726–5745-116">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> The Fillmores had planned a tour of the South after they had left the White House, but Abigail caught a cold at President Pierce's inauguration, developed pneumonia, and died in Washington on March 30, 1853. A saddened Fillmore returned to Buffalo for the burial.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback5858–5865_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback5858–5865-117">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The fact that he was in mourning limited his social activities, and he made ends meet on the income from his investments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6025–6031_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6025–6031-118">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> He was bereaved again on July 26, 1854, when his only daughter, Mary, died of <a href="/wiki/Cholera" title="Cholera">cholera</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Subsequent_political_activity">Subsequent political activity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Subsequent political activity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The former president ended his seclusion in early 1854, as a debate over Senator Douglas's <a href="/wiki/Kansas%E2%80%93Nebraska_Act" title="Kansas–Nebraska Act">Kansas–Nebraska Bill</a> embroiled the nation. The bill would open the northern portion of the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a> to settlement and end the northern limit on slavery under the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a> of 1820. Fillmore retained many supporters, planned an ostensibly nonpolitical national tour, and privately rallied disaffected Whig politicians to preserve the Union and to back him in a run for president. Fillmore made public appearances opening railroads and visiting the grave of Senator Clay but met with politicians outside the public eye during the late winter and the spring of 1854.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6038–6057_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6038–6057-120">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Such a comeback could not be under the auspices of the Whig Party, with its remnants divided by the Kansas–Nebraska legislation, which passed with the support of Pierce. Many northern foes of slavery, such as Seward, gravitated toward the new <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican Party</a>, but Fillmore saw no home for himself there. In the early 1850s, there was considerable hostility toward immigrants, especially <a href="/wiki/Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholics">Catholics</a>, who had recently arrived in the United States in large numbers, and several nativist organizations, including the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Star_Spangled_Banner" title="Order of the Star Spangled Banner">Order of the Star Spangled Banner</a>, sprang up in reaction. By 1854 the order had morphed into the American Party, which became known as the <a href="/wiki/Know_Nothings" class="mw-redirect" title="Know Nothings">Know Nothings</a>. (In its early days, members were sworn to keep its internal deliberations private and, if asked, were to say they knew nothing about them.)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback5900–5966_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback5900–5966-121">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many from Fillmore's "National Whig" faction had joined the Know Nothings by 1854 and influenced the organization to take up causes besides nativism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback5952–5959_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback5952–5959-122">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore was encouraged by the success of the Know Nothings in the 1854 midterm elections in which they won in several states of the Northeast and showed strength in the South. On January 1, 1855, he sent a letter for publication that warned against immigrant influence in American elections, and he soon joined the order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith252–253_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith252–253-123">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later that year Fillmore went abroad, and stated publicly that as he lacked office he might as well travel. The trip was at the advice of political friends, who felt that by touring he would avoid involvement in the contentious issues of the day. He spent over a year, from March 1855 to June 1856, in Europe and the Middle East. <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> is said to have pronounced the ex-president as the handsomest man she had ever seen, and his coincidental appearance with Van Buren in the gallery of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> provoked a comment from the MP <a href="/wiki/John_Bright" title="John Bright">John Bright</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6191–6234_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6191–6234-124">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Dorothea_Dix" title="Dorothea Dix">Dorothea Dix</a> had preceded him to Europe and was lobbying to improve conditions for the <a href="/wiki/Mentally_ill" class="mw-redirect" title="Mentally ill">mentally ill</a>. They continued to correspond and met several times.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnyder217–218_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder217–218-125">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> In Rome, Fillmore had an <a href="/wiki/Papal_audience" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal audience">audience</a> with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius&#160;IX</a>. He carefully weighed the political pros and cons of meeting with Pius. He nearly withdrew from the meeting when he was told that he would have to kneel and kiss the Pope's hand. To avoid that, Pius remained seated throughout the meeting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6248_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6248-126">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman132_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman132-127">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Portrait of Andrew Donelson" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/170px-Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/255px-Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/340px-Appletons%27_Donelson_Andrew_Jackson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="515" data-file-height="617" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Andrew_Donelson" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrew Donelson">Andrew Donelson</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1856_campaign">1856 campaign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: 1856 campaign">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/1856_United_States_presidential_election" title="1856 United States presidential election">1856 United States presidential election</a>, <a href="/wiki/1856_American_National_Convention" title="1856 American National Convention">1856 American National Convention</a>, and <a href="/wiki/1856_Whig_National_Convention" title="1856 Whig National Convention">1856 Whig National Convention</a></div>
<p>Fillmore's allies were in full control of the American Party and arranged for him to get its presidential nomination while he was in Europe. The <a href="/wiki/1856_American_National_Convention" title="1856 American National Convention">Know Nothing convention</a> chose Fillmore's running mate: <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Donelson" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrew Donelson">Andrew Donelson</a> of Kentucky, the nephew by marriage and once-ward of President Jackson. Fillmore made a celebrated return in June 1856 by speaking at a series of welcomes, which began with his arrival at a huge reception in New York City and continued across the state to Buffalo. The addresses were portrayed as expressions of thanks for his reception, rather than as campaign speeches, which might be considered illicit office-seeking if they were made by a presidential hopeful. Fillmore warned that electing the Republican candidate, former California senator <a href="/wiki/John_C._Fr%C3%A9mont" title="John C. Frémont">John C. Frémont</a>, who had no support in the South, would divide the Union and lead to civil war. Both Fillmore and the Democratic candidate, former Pennsylvania senator <a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan" title="James Buchanan">James Buchanan</a>, agreed that slavery was principally a matter for the states, not the federal government. Fillmore rarely spoke about the immigration question, focused on the sectional divide, and urged the preservation of the Union.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry6650–6699_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry6650–6699-128">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6326–6411_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6326–6411-129">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1856.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Map showing results of the 1856 election" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>Fillmore won only Maryland (pink).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Once Fillmore was back home in Buffalo, he had no excuse to make speeches, and his campaign stagnated through the summer and the fall of 1856. Political fixers who had been Whigs, such as Weed, tended to join the Republican Party, and the Know Nothings lacked experience at selling anything but nativism. Accordingly, Fillmore's pro-Union stance mostly went unheard. Although the South was friendly towards Fillmore, many people feared that a Frémont victory would lead to secession, and some of those who were sympathetic to Fillmore moved into the Buchanan camp for fear of splitting the anti-Frémont vote, which might elect the Republican.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6398–6458_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6398–6458-130">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Scarry suggested that the events of 1856, including <a href="/wiki/Bleeding_Kansas" title="Bleeding Kansas">the conflict</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kansas_Territory" title="Kansas Territory">Kansas Territory</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Caning_of_Charles_Sumner" title="Caning of Charles Sumner">caning of Charles Sumner</a> on the floor of the Senate, polarized the nation and made Fillmore's moderate stance obsolete.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry6918_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry6918-131">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan won with 1,836,072 votes (45.3%) and 174 electoral votes to Frémont's 1,342,345 votes (33.1%) and 114 electoral votes. Fillmore and Donelson finished third by winning 873,053 votes (21.6%) and carrying the state of Maryland and its eight electoral votes.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;j&#93;</a></sup> The American Party ticket narrowly lost in several southern states, and a change of fewer than 8,000 votes in Louisiana, Kentucky, and Tennessee would have thrown the election to the House of Representatives, where the sectional divide would have made the outcome uncertain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6458–6473_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6458–6473-134">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The historian <a href="/wiki/Allan_Nevins" title="Allan Nevins">Allan Nevins</a> wrote that Fillmore was not a Know Nothing or a nativist, offering as support that Fillmore was out of the country when the nomination came and had not been consulted about running. Nevins stated about Fillmore that "by no spoken or written word had he indicated a subscription to American tenets."<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> However, Fillmore had sent a letter for publication in 1855 that explicitly denounced immigrant influence in elections<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith252–253_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith252–253-123">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> and Fillmore stated that the American Party was the "only hope of forming a truly national party, which shall ignore this constant and distracting agitation of slavery."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography_2-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmerican_National_Biography-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Remarriage.2C_later_life.2C_and_death"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Remarriage,_later_life,_and_death">Remarriage, later life, and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Remarriage, later life, and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Caroline_Fillmore_(Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Portrait of Caroline Fillmore looking to her right" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg/170px-Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg/255px-Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg/340px-Caroline_Fillmore_%28Wife_of_President_Millard_Fillmore%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="492" data-file-height="550" /></a><figcaption>Caroline Fillmore</figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore considered his political career to have ended with his defeat in 1856. He again felt inhibited from returning to the practice of law. But his financial worries ended on February 10, 1858, when he married <a href="/wiki/Caroline_C._Fillmore" title="Caroline C. Fillmore">Caroline McIntosh</a>, a well-to-do widow. Their combined wealth allowed them to purchase a large house on <a href="/wiki/Niagara_Square" title="Niagara Square">Niagara Square</a> in Buffalo, where they lived for the remainder of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6476–6518_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6476–6518-137">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> There, the Fillmores devoted themselves to entertaining and philanthropy. According to the historian Smith, "They generously supported almost every conceivable cause."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith254–255_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith254–255-138">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Among these were the <a href="/wiki/Kaleida_Health" title="Kaleida Health">Buffalo General Hospital</a>, which he helped found.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length,_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of Fillmore from the waist up" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg/170px-Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg/255px-Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg/340px-Millard_Fillmore._Waist_length%2C_seated_-_NARA_-_530497.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2160" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Fillmore during the Civil War</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election" title="1860 United States presidential election">1860 presidential election</a> Fillmore voted for Senator Douglas, the Democratic nominee. After the vote, in which the Republican nominee, former Illinois Representative <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>, was elected, many sought out Fillmore's views, but he refused to take any part in the secession crisis that followed, feeling that he lacked influence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry7285–7297_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry7285–7297-140">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> He decried Buchanan's inaction as states left the Union and wrote that although the federal government could not coerce a state, those advocating secession should be regarded as traitors. When Lincoln came to Buffalo en route to his inauguration, Fillmore led the committee selected to receive him, hosted him at his mansion, and took him to church.
</p><p>Once the Civil War began, Fillmore supported Lincoln's to preserve the Union.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6578–6600_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6578–6600-141">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> He commanded the <a href="/wiki/Union_Continentals" title="Union Continentals">Union Continentals</a>, a corps of <a href="/wiki/Militia" title="Militia">home guards</a> of men over 45 from <a href="/wiki/Upstate_New_York" title="Upstate New York">upstate New York</a>. The Continentals trained to defend the Buffalo area in the event of a Confederate attack. They performed military drills and ceremonial functions at parades, funerals, and other events. The Union Continentals guarded Lincoln's funeral train in Buffalo. They continued operations after the war, and Fillmore remained active with them almost until his death.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith264–265_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith264–265-143">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite Fillmore's zeal in the war effort, he gave a speech in early 1864 calling for magnanimity toward the South after the war and counted its heavy cost, both in finances and in blood. The Lincoln administration saw the speech as an attack on it that could not be tolerated in an election year, and Fillmore was criticized in many newspapers and was called a <a href="/wiki/Copperhead_(politics)" title="Copperhead (politics)">Copperhead</a> and even a traitor. That led to lasting ill-feeling against Fillmore in many circles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6667–6706_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6667–6706-144">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1864_United_States_presidential_election" title="1864 United States presidential election">1864 presidential election</a>, Fillmore supported the Democratic nominee, <a href="/wiki/George_B._McClellan" title="George B. McClellan">George B. McClellan</a>, since he believed that the Democratic Party's plan for immediate cessation of fighting and allowing the seceded states to return with slavery intact was the best chance to restore the Union.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After <a href="/wiki/Lincoln_assassination" class="mw-redirect" title="Lincoln assassination">Lincoln's assassination</a> in April 1865, black ink was thrown on Fillmore's house because it was not draped in mourning like others. Fillmore was apparently out of town at the time and put black drapes in the windows once he returned. He retained his position as Buffalo's leading citizen and was among those selected to escort the body when Lincoln's funeral train passed through Buffalo, but many remained angry at him for his wartime positions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6706_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6706-146">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore supported President <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Reconstruction_era_of_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Reconstruction era of the United States">Reconstruction</a> policies since he felt that the nation needed to be reconciled as quickly as possible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman154_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman154-147">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> He devoted most of his time to civic activities. He aided Buffalo in becoming the third American city to have a permanent art gallery, the <a href="/wiki/Buffalo_Fine_Arts_Academy" class="mw-redirect" title="Buffalo Fine Arts Academy">Buffalo Fine Arts Academy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6783–6790_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6783–6790-148">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>Fillmore stayed in good health almost to the end of his life. He suffered a stroke in February 1874, and died on March 8, at age 74, after suffering a second stroke. Two days later, he was buried at Forest Lawn Cemetery in Buffalo after a funeral procession including hundreds of others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6930–6946_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6930–6946-149">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. Senate sent three of its members to honor Fillmore, including former vice president <a href="/wiki/Hannibal_Hamlin" title="Hannibal Hamlin">Hannibal Hamlin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry8118_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry8118-150">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_historical_view">Legacy and historical view</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Legacy and historical view">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Millard_Fillmore_$1_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Sketch of the obverse side of Millard Fillmore&#39;s presidential dollar coin" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg/150px-Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg/225px-Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg/300px-Millard_Fillmore_%241_Presidential_Coin_obverse_sketch.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1092" data-file-height="1092" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Presidential_$1_Coin_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidential $1 Coin Program">Presidential dollar</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Fillmore is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">ranked by historians and political scientists</a> as one of the worst U.S. presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-151">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> His handling of major political issues, such as slavery, has led many historians to describe him as weak and inept.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-151">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> According to biographer Scarry: "No president of the United States<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... has suffered as much ridicule as Millard Fillmore."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry8151_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry8151-152">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Scarry ascribes much of the abuse to a tendency to denigrate the presidents who served in the years just before the Civil War as lacking in leadership. For example, President <a href="/wiki/Harry_S._Truman" title="Harry S. Truman">Harry S. Truman</a> later "characterized Fillmore as a weak, trivial thumb-twaddler who would do nothing to offend anyone" and as partially responsible for the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry8157–8161_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry8157–8161-153">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Fillmore's name has become a byword in popular culture for easily forgotten and inconsequential presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-bostonglobe_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bostonglobe-154">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In 2010, Anna Prior wrote in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal" title="The Wall Street Journal">The Wall Street Journal</a></i> that Fillmore's very name connotes mediocrity.<sup id="cite_ref-wsj_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wsj-155">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Finkelman, another Fillmore biographer, comments, "on the central issues of the age his vision was myopic and his legacy is worse<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... in the end, Fillmore was always on the wrong side of the great moral and political issues."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFinkelman137_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelman137-156">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> But Rayback applauds "the warmth and wisdom with which he had defended the Union",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback6953_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback6953-157">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> and Smith finds Fillmore "a conscientious president" who honored his oath of office by enforcing the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Fugitive Slave Act">Fugitive Slave Act</a> rather than govern based on his personal preferences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith257,_260_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith257,_260-158">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Steven_G._Calabresi" class="mw-redirect" title="Steven G. Calabresi">Steven G. Calabresi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christopher_S._Yoo" class="mw-redirect" title="Christopher S. Yoo">Christopher S. Yoo</a>, in their study of presidential power, deem Fillmore "a faithful executor of the laws of the United States&#160;&#8211;&#32;for good and for ill".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalabresi_&amp;_Yoo151_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalabresi_&amp;_Yoo151-159">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> But according to Smith, the enforcement of the Act has given Fillmore an undeserved pro-southern reputation. Fillmore's place in history has also suffered because "even those who give him high marks for his support of the compromise have done so almost grudgingly, probably because of his Know-Nothing candidacy in 1856."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith260–261_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith260–261-160">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Smith argues that Fillmore's association with the Know Nothings looks far worse in retrospect than at the time and that Fillmore was not motivated by <a href="/wiki/Nativism_in_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Nativism in the United States">nativism</a> in his candidacy,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith254_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith254-161">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> despite the letter he wrote for publication that stoked fear about immigrant influence in elections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith252–253_123-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith252–253-123">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_Gravesite,_Buffalo,_NY.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of the pink obelisk marking Fillmore&#39;s grave" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg/170px-Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="276" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg/255px-Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg/340px-Fillmore_Gravesite%2C_Buffalo%2C_NY.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1121" data-file-height="1819" /></a><figcaption>A pink obelisk marks Fillmore's grave at <a href="/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York" title="Buffalo, New York">Buffalo</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Forest_Lawn_Cemetery_(Buffalo)" title="Forest Lawn Cemetery (Buffalo)">Forest Lawn Cemetery</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Benson Lee Grayson suggests that the Fillmore administration's ability to avoid potential problems is too often overlooked. Fillmore's constant attention to Mexico avoided a resumption of the Mexican–American War and laid the groundwork for the <a href="/wiki/Gadsden_Purchase" title="Gadsden Purchase">Gadsden Treaty</a> during Pierce's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrayson120_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrayson120-162">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The Fillmore administration also resolved a controversy with Portugal left over from the Taylor administration;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrayson83_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrayson83-163">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> smoothed over a disagreement with Peru over <a href="/wiki/List_of_Guano_Island_claims" title="List of Guano Island claims">guano islands</a>; and peacefully resolved disputes with Britain, France, and Spain over Cuba. All these crises were resolved without the United States going to war or losing face.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrayson103–109_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrayson103–109-164">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Grayson also applauds Fillmore's firm stand against Texas's ambitions in New Mexico during the 1850 crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith288–289_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith288–289-165">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Fred_I._Greenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Fred I. Greenstein">Fred I. Greenstein</a> and Dale Anderson praise Fillmore for his resolve in his early months in office and note that Fillmore "is typically described as stolid, bland, and conventional, but such terms underestimate the forcefulness evinced by his handling of the Texas-New Mexico border crisis, his decision to replace Taylor's entire cabinet, and his effectiveness in advancing the Compromise of 1850."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson55_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenstein_&amp;_Anderson55-166">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Political scientist <a href="/wiki/James_E._Campbell_(academic)" title="James E. Campbell (academic)">James E. Campbell</a> defends Fillmore's legacy, writing, "Historians have underrated him, his detractors have unfairly maligned him, and the institutions he honorably served have disrespected him". Campbell argues that the Compromise of 1850 "did more good than harm for the nation and the <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">anti-slavery cause</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fillmore and his wife, Abigail, established the first White House library.<sup id="cite_ref-abigailfillmore_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abigailfillmore-168">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> There are a number of remembrances of Fillmore; his East Aurora house still stands, and sites honor him at his birthplace and boyhood home, where the Millard Fillmore Memorial Association dedicated a replica log cabin in 1963.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERayback8151–8157_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERayback8151–8157-169">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> A statue of Fillmore stands outside <a href="/wiki/Buffalo_City_Hall" title="Buffalo City Hall">Buffalo City Hall</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScarry6946–6953_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScarry6946–6953-170">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> At the university he helped found, now the <a href="/wiki/University_at_Buffalo" title="University at Buffalo">University at Buffalo</a>, Millard Fillmore Academic Center and Millard Fillmore College bear his name.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> On February 18, 2010, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Mint" title="United States Mint">United States Mint</a> released the 13th coin in the <a href="/wiki/Presidential_$1_Coin_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidential $1 Coin Program">Presidential $1 Coin Program</a>, bearing Fillmore's likeness.<sup id="cite_ref-wsj_155-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wsj-155">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the assessment of Fillmore by the <a href="/wiki/Miller_Center_of_Public_Affairs" title="Miller Center of Public Affairs">Miller Center of Public Affairs</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Any assessment of a President who served a century and a half ago must be refracted through a consideration of the interesting times in which he lived. Fillmore's political career encompassed the tortuous course toward the two-party system that we know today. The Whigs were not cohesive enough to survive the slavery imbroglio, while parties like the Anti-Masonics and Know-Nothings were too extremist. When, as President, Fillmore sided with proslavery elements in ordering enforcement of the Fugitive Slave Law, he all but guaranteed that he would be the last Whig President. The first modern two-party system of Whigs and Democrats had succeeded only in dividing the nation in two by the 1850s, and seven years later, the election of the first Republican President, Abraham Lincoln, would guarantee civil war.</p></blockquote>
<div class="noprint"><ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Statue by Bryant Baker at Buffalo City Hall, Buffalo, New York, 1930"><img alt="Statue by Bryant Baker at Buffalo City Hall, Buffalo, New York, 1930" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg/124px-Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg" decoding="async" width="124" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg/187px-Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg/249px-Millard_Fillmore_by_Bryant_Baker.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1867" data-file-height="3000" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p>Statue by <a href="/wiki/Bryant_Baker" title="Bryant Baker">Bryant Baker</a> at Buffalo City Hall, Buffalo, New York, 1930
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_Home_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Fillmore&#39;s East Aurora house was moved off Main Street."><img alt="Fillmore&#39;s East Aurora house was moved off Main Street." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Fillmore_Home_2.jpg/200px-Fillmore_Home_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Fillmore_Home_2.jpg/300px-Fillmore_Home_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Fillmore_Home_2.jpg/400px-Fillmore_Home_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p>Fillmore's East Aurora house was moved off Main Street.
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="The house is designated a National Historic Landmark."><img alt="The house is designated a National Historic Landmark." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg/200px-Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg/300px-Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg/400px-Fillmore_House_NHL.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">

</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_DAR.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="The DAR placed this plaque on the house in 1931."><img alt="The DAR placed this plaque on the house in 1931." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Fillmore_DAR.jpg/200px-Fillmore_DAR.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Fillmore_DAR.jpg/300px-Fillmore_DAR.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Fillmore_DAR.jpg/400px-Fillmore_DAR.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Daughters_of_the_American_Revolution" title="Daughters of the American Revolution">DAR</a> placed this plaque on the house in 1931.
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="A memorial to Fillmore on the gate surrounding his plot in Buffalo"><img alt="A memorial to Fillmore on the gate surrounding his plot in Buffalo" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg/200px-Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg/300px-Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg/400px-Fillmore_plot_plaque.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">

</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Detail of the Fillmore obelisk in Buffalo"><img alt="Detail of the Fillmore obelisk in Buffalo" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg/150px-Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg/225px-Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg/300px-Fillmore_obelisk_detail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1704" data-file-height="2272" /></a></span></div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Millard_Fillmore&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Fillmore was Vice President under President <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> and became President upon Taylor's death on July 9, 1850. Prior to adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment" class="mw-redirect" title="Twenty-fifth Amendment">Twenty-fifth Amendment</a> (1967) a vacancy in the office of vice president was not filled until the next ensuing election and inauguration.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Nathaniel Fillmore, the first father of a President to visit his son at the White House, told a questioner how to raise a son to be president: "Cradle him in a sap trough."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnyder50_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder50-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-29">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Fillmore's uncle <a href="/wiki/Calvin_Fillmore" title="Calvin Fillmore">Calvin Fillmore</a> served in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Assembly" title="New York State Assembly">New York State Assembly</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> another uncle, Simeon Fillmore, served as <a href="/wiki/Town_supervisor" class="mw-redirect" title="Town supervisor">town supervisor</a> of <a href="/wiki/Clarence,_New_York" title="Clarence, New York">Clarence, New York</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Centennial_History_of_Erie_County,_New_York&#39;&#39;_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Centennial_History_of_Erie_County,_New_York&#39;&#39;-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-77"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-77">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">South Carolina did not yet use the popular vote for choosing electors, with the legislature electing them instead.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-80"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-80">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Until 1913 senators were elected by the state legislatures, not by the people.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-83">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The modern-day states of New Mexico and Arizona, less the <a href="/wiki/Gadsden_Purchase" title="Gadsden Purchase">Gadsden Purchase</a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-87"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-87">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The U.S. Constitution designates the vice president as the Senate's presiding officer.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-89"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-89">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The term derives from the transportation vehicle, as the bill carries all the related proposals as "passengers".</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-97"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-97">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Calhoun was dead, Webster was Secretary of State, and Clay was absent since he was recovering in <a href="/wiki/Newport,_Rhode_Island" title="Newport, Rhode Island">Newport, Rhode Island</a> from his exertions on behalf of the bill.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-133"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-133">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Fillmore thus became the first former president to receive electoral votes, a distinction that later also included <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a> (1892) and <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> (1912).<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
